My dear Mr. Hull,
This is a plea that steps be taken by govern-
m
ment to embargo materials usable in modern war to Japan.
I feel it is now proven that we have been "Japan's
894.24/444
294.24
best partner". It is plain that our machine tools are
vital to her, in making her mmitions. We have evidence
that war-metal bearing Amerioan names have been found in
dead Chinese soldiers, and that American truoks for carry-
ing on the advance are in use in China by Japan.
Up to the point when the Chinese could receive
goods through Honkkong and Canton, the oruelty to the
Chinese of invoking our neutrality aot may have seemed
to some too muoh. Now the plain faot is that they oan-
not reoeive, even if they oan buy.
Is it not time to explore the question of a
presidential embargo?
I am sure you receive delegations from many
quarters in behalf of one or another portion of striomen
humanity on the globe. I as a minister must face four
Amerioans just back from Shanghai, on a Sunday morning,
their owm life projeot as teachers there now gone, and
their faces drawn with what of the horror they have seen.
May I conclude with an expression of my very
F/A
deep regret that you had to deoline our invitation again
this year to address the Conneotiout Counoil of Churohes
on November 14 in Hartford, at our Institute? As president
of the Counoil, I am very sorry that we cannot have the
benefit and profit of your presence.
Yours sincerely,
Menile 7. Claan
Morrill F. Clarke
The Hon. Cordell Hull,
The Department of State,
Washington, D.C.

sn
My Dear Sirs:
sinatures
to a protest against the sending of scrap iron and other
war material to Japan. The Peace Committee of San Diego
and the W.I.L.P.F. seeing that on the one hand the State
Department and American people in general deplore the in-
vasion of China by Japan, the slaughter of the Chinese and
the violation of a world obligation to sustain such a moral
integrity in the Comity of Nations as would not tolerate, or
lend aid and comfort to such lawless proceedures, while, on
the other hand the means for prosecuting these outrageous
policies are being furnished to Japan by those private in-
terests which profit by this woeful trade, do here submit
for your consideration these protests against such inconsis-
tent practices.
We believe that if the State Department had the
power to apply so unjustly the provisions of the Neutrality
Act in the case of wartorn Spain, the State Department can
find a way to halt this trade in those materials which are
used to murder the Chinese people and make possible the sei-
zure of their homeland.
We believe that if we were properly organized
millions of American citizens would sign these protests, pre
fering to see our scrap iron dumped into the cean where the
bitter brew of its unworthy sustenance of evil purposes would
be dissipated, than to have this scrap iron used to paralyze
and destroy the great Chinese race.
F
We further declare that we make these protests
/WB
fully aware of the complexities involved in policies designed
to halt shipments of scrap iron to Japan, and with full appro-
bation of all the trade and "Good Neighbor" policies of the
Administration seeking to establish friendly world trade routes.

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.96)
believe however that an intelligent application to such a
oroblem will result in solutions which wiil mitigate or end
the inconsistences of our present actions.
We make these protests not only with no ill-will
toward the Japanese people, but with a desire to see them work
out their destiny through cooperative trade enterprise and
legitimate avenues of growth and expansion. We humbly acknow-
ledge our own share of guilt in helping to equip Japan with
the present means of conquest through violence.
Mr. President and Honorable Secretary of State we
again earnestly submit these and other like signatures we may
obtain for your consideration. We ask for some response which
will adequately cover the matter of our protest.
Respectfally submitted,
Pherman
Chairman of
Peace Cimmittu
late H. hDeving
C.Wmen' It Lague PF
Address
Peace Committee,
3546 First Avenue,
San Diego, California.

Hon. Franklin D. Roosevelt

Washington, D.C.
894.24/314
My dear Mr. President:
It was exceedingly gracious of you to reply so carefully to my
inquiry as to why you had not applied the Neutrality Act to Japan and
China in the present conflict. Your letter confirms my confidenoe in
your solicitous conoern for China, and, together with the material en-
closed, makes it entirely clear that you do not regard this as the most
effective method of giving assistance.
May I presume further upon your proven interest in this problem,
not again by urging a particular course of action, but by commenting.
from the standpoint of one long resident in North China, upon the super-
lative opportunity you have for influencing the outcome of hostilities.
to the advantage not only of China, but ultimately of our own country as
894.24/449
well, and of the greeter cause of international security.
If I may refer again to that memorable interview with you in the
spring of 1933, you made the remark that no time need be wasted over the
moral aspects of the issue. This is immeasurably more true now than then.
Not only is the invasion of China by Japan wholly indefensible, but the
ruthless barbarity with which it is being conduo ed can soaroely be exag-
gerated. Suffice it to state that the devastation, looting, raping, and
slaughter of civilians which are generally known to have taken place in
Nanking have oocurred in varying degrees wherever the Japanese troops have
gone, with the exception of Peking and a few port cities where they en-
countered virtually no resistance. Even in these places there is cruel
oppression and disregard of elemental human rights.
What is of more practical importance, however, is the evidenoe w who
live here have that the Japanese military leaders will be very tenaoious
in their control of North China, releasing their iron grip only under.
irresistible compulsion. They are planning permanent occupation andthe
exploitation of its resources for their own people, virtually as a oelonial
possession. Its inhabitants will suffer the fate of those in Korea and
Manchuria as rapidly as the customary procedure for repressive measures
can be put into effect. The aim and result will be intellectual and economio
serfdom. That such an outcome will be accepted by the present.offioials of
F/A

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.98)
* 2 *
the National Government and the people of the unoccupied areas no one
who is familiar with their newly-aroused patriotic determination oan be
made to believe. Even the leaders of the "puppet govornment", and those
who for fear or under economic pressure have taken positions in it, share -
with almost negligible exceptions - in this attitude, and it is more true
of the common people of this region than ever before perhaps in Chinese
history. This means a prolonged struggle, disastrous to all concerned,
or at its worst an embittered but impotent submission under military coercion
insofar as that can be maintained. There is unmistakable evidenoe also that
all foreign interests that conflict with those of the Japanese or are not
amenable to their control will be forcibly eliminated. The fear is wide-
spread among well-informed Chinese that Great Britain might be induced to
accept a compromise settlement by which Japan would withdraw from the rest
of China if unthwarted in her designs in these five northern provinces.
You have again rendered a notable service to humanity by your messages
to European leaders when, as it would seem, nothing else could have averted
war. With the almost unanimous American sympathy for China, could you not
give expression to this in some no less significant form? It is not im-
possible that by the time this reac!cs you even the Japanese militarists
would welcome the proposal of mediation or of a conference, possibly with
the intimation that sterner measures might be enforced, if this were not
accepted. We who are living close to the conflict cannot conceive any such
action as an embargo on the shipment of war materials to Japan as involving
the slightest danger of war against the United States. That it would almost
certainly mean reprisals against American property in this area, quite
possibly also the loss of life, would be a small sacrifice in comparison
with the immense gain to this suffering people and to the cause of inter-
national justice. Nor should this danger act to the slightest degree as
a deterrent. Speaking for myself and the particular American enterprise
I represent, we should not ask that any fear for the safety of our lives
or this prouerty should deter you in taking such strong action as you may
deem wise. I find that other Americans with similar responsibilities here
feel as I do.
More constructively, and with a long look into the future, American
assistance to China in her present agony and the assertion of our deter-
mination to help maintain her sovereignty and administrative and territorial
integrity would most probably result in securing her national independence.
Delivered at last from the menace of Japanese military domination, this vast
and virile population would most naturally develop into a unified democracy
sharing our own finest ideals of freedom  nd of international peace, based
on reason and right. China would thus become an enormous asset in the grim
struggle to preserve these values against unrestrained aggression growing
more arrogant and rapacious with each successful use of the modern science
of mechanized slaughter. It is because I covet for you, of whose desire for
the welfare of China I am more than ever assured, the leadership in this
supremely worth while effort that I have ventured to impose on your patience
at suoh length.
Very respectfully yours,
heinxewStcark

Dear Mr. Hull:
m
I have just been reading the column by Nalter Lippmann in the
Saturday Herald Tribune in which he com:ents on the uanger that
our stron? policy insisting on the open aocr in China may lead us
into antagonism from Great britain who, he alleges, has decided
to recognize that Jepan has won the Far  stern war und to col-
laborate with her program of rehabilitation. I um inclined to
tnink that Mr. Lip: muinn exaggerates the col apse of Caina, the
strength of the Japanese position, and the degree to which Great
Britain has committed hersolf to supporting Japan. One cannot
ignore the possibility that tne iocmy picture he :gints may
prove to be true, but I t ink he is somowhat premature and there
is still a possibility or saving the rituation in the rer ast,
and through that, ivin a new cor.fidence to democracy taroughout
8
the world.
94.24/450
It is for tnis reason that I add my veice to many which y u douot-
less heve heerd thet steps be immediately taken to induce Congress
to pess en  ct authori  ing the executive to place an embargo on
994.24
war materials, including particularly coprer, iron, and petroleum,
to Jepan, and also upon loans to thst country, that with authori-
zation, to imvose such an embargo, the executive immediately enter
into negotiations vith Great Britain, France, and the other Nine
Power Treaty countries to embark upon a policy implementing the
Nine Power Treaty and the Pact of Paris in accordance vith the
general principles laid down et the Brussels Conference last
year. Fossibly a renewal of the Erussels Conference would be
necessary to carry this out.
I am of course aware thet the response of public opinion in this
country to the President's "quarantine" address a year agos not
such as to justify immediate strong action in the Far ast. It
seems to me, however, that the situation has somewhat changed now.
The disclosure that American industry is supplying 54 per cent
of the essential imported war materials to Japan and the activities
of the American committee against participetion in Japen's war of
F/A
aggression have aroused American public opinion to a sense of the
inconsistency of our profess ons end practice in regard to the

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.99)
The Honorable Cordell Hull
-2-
November 8, 1938
Far East. I noticed that the Gallup Poll indiceted that public
opinion in this country is overwhelmingly pro-Chinese and that
less than one per cent of those sampled by this poll favored
Japan. While many of those favorable to China were and may be
still alarmed at the dengers of a policy of embargoes against
Japan, I think there is less alarm on this score than there wes
a year ago. Japan has extended her military operations very
greatly and her financial situation is not at all satisfactory.
She is undoubtedly attempting to convince the world that her
recent captures of Canton and Hankow have brought the war to
an end, but people acquainted with the Chinese situation do not
seem to agree, and think that Chinese resistance through guerrilla
tactics can be protracted indefinitely unless France and Great
Britain shut off the supply of arms to China through Indo China
and Burma.
It is a possibility that Europeen powers, having begun a policy of
submitting to the dictators in Euro pe, may continue this policy
by assisting Jepan to get what she wants in the Far ast. It may
be as Mr. Lippmann suggests that the British zovernment is already
embarked upon this policy, but it seems possible that a strong
initiative by the United States would still be able to induce
the British and French to respect their treaty obligations in
the Far East and to go along with an American policy of embargoes.
We must recall that the League of Nations Assembly in its Sep-
tember session definitely authorized members of the League to
engage in sanctions against Japan.
It has repeatedly been pointed out that effective embargoes against
Japan might induce that country to seize the oil fields in the
Dutch East Indies, perhaps taking the Philippines on the way.
That possibility should doubtless be guarded against by the basing
of an adequate number of naval vessels at Manila and Singapore
and unless Great Britain would collaborate in such a policy perhaps
it should not be embarked upon. If that were done, however, it
seems to me the danger of Japenese retaliation would be reduced to
a minimum. No one seriously thinks Japan would attempt to attack
the United States on our side of the Pacific. In its present com-
mitments in China, it seems unlikely that its navy would risk an
attack on the Philippines or the Dutch Indies if adequate naval
preparations were made by the democracies in that vicinity.
It is obvious that unless adequate methods are taken to implement
the policy which we asserted a year ago in recognizing that Japan
had gone to war contrary to treaties and in the recent note
pointing out the serious violations of the open door by Jepan in
China, we may find ourselves in the position of beating an igno-
minious retreat. Immediate action is necessary if this situation
is to be saved.

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.98)
The Honorable Cordell Hull
-3-
November 8, 1938
But I think one can realize that more than the Far Eastern situation
is at stake. Democracy is retreating in Europe before the dictators
and it appears that n body except ourselves is in a position to stop
the demoralization of the forces of liberty and humanity against
military aggression and to rehabilitate respect for international
law and treaties.fcollaboration between the United States and Great
Britain could be effectagain in the Far East as it was in l922,
and that is certainly the area where opinion in this country would
most definitely support a vigorous collaborative policy, The tide
of defeatism which is sweeping the democracies may be stemmed.
Of course I realize that these consideretions ere steadily before you
and ycur colleagues in the Department, but I also realize your
sense of dependence upon puhlic opinion in the country and am adding
my voice as one evidence of the present state of public opinion.
Sincerely yours,
7
Quincy Wright

My dear Mr. Hull,

licenses for export of arms , eto., to

I am, however, still unable to follow
the argument that the refusal to export to
Japan the materials for her conquest would
lead us into war. There is very little of
ours in China which Japan has not either
destroyed already or jeopardized. And I
cannot conceive of her seriously starting
an armed conflict with us, either near the
Phillippines or oloser.
Meantime I find myself active in

approaohing for large gifts some whose Con-
neotiout factories have supplied machine tools
eto to the Japanese who are causing the suffer-

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.93)
SOUTH CHURCH
ANDOVER. MASSACHUSETTS
FOUNDED 1711
WhmAs, the people of the United States desire peace and deplore international
lawlessness and aggression, and
WHEREAS, the Japanese invasion of China, fo mented by the military party of Japan.
has led the United States of America and many other nations to indict Japan as
e violator of the Mine-Power Treaty and the Kellogg pact, and to protest re-
peatedly agminst the merciless bombing of undefended Chinese cities, the unnecy
sary slaughter of Chinese civilians, and the destruction and imperilment of schools,
churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in which their nationals are engaged.
and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are being used to manu-
facture weapons of war in Japan, that American planes are being used to carry
bombs from America for the destruction of Chinese civilian populations, that shells
from America are loading Japanese guns, and that America is supplying to Japan huge
quantities of scrap iron, oil, trucks, and other materials used in the furtherance
of the war, over and above the amount of our normal trade of recent years, and
WHEREAs, we sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire to build up an
independent, democratic state, an end gravely threatened by the present aggression
of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it possible for certain American
interests to profit from the wanton destruction of the innocent victims of Japan-
ese aggression,
THERErORE. we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the United
States and Congress, in accordance with the principle of the Good Neighbor and
in the interests of the people of the United States, China and Japan, to take
every practicable means, direct or indirect, to bring to an end a traffic from our
country which is compelling us to be a partner in the destruction of the Chinese
people.

Arthur dr Bassett.

"HOSPITAL OF UNIVERSAL LOVE"
(Methodist General Hespital)
Hankew China. July 26,1938
ome of the refreshments of mind and seul after a terrible week
a seed mail an Sunday. On,how I would like to have been with
In Kashmir! The very thought of it seems like a werld apart from
a this war herror.
After seme weeks of rain and ne raids,we have had a fortnight of
ghastly maids. One very near us last Tuesday with a thousand casualties
amengst the peer and amether that struak very near the Ameriean Chureh
Missien Bespital in Wuchang across the river Yangste. I went aeress to
see if I eeuld help Miss Reiley there. A maternity hospital opened by the
givermment was struek. The bomb went through three floors of reinforeed
cenesete,killing or wounding all the inmates in it.Some of the mothers
lay se peaseful in death with their babies slasped in their arms,whike
the reseue squad were trying to get bodlies out from beneath the debris.
It seemed as though they were happier than the ones who were so griev-
ously wounded.One six months pregnant wonan had a ghastly wound of the
abdemen,with rupture of the uterus,and a long slit in the buttook of the
ttetus.Did ever sueh devil's work darken civilization before!
Last Tuesday the bombs dropped so near us that the bombers
passed over our heads,we waited in a tense stillness not kmowing what
a mement might bring.But on,so glad to be able to rest one's heart in
our loving Lord's care and feel Him near.Then we heard the thud,thud of
bembs falling,and then the explesions,and our hearts were pounding,know-
ing that some poor folk were being killed somewhere near.A Guild Hall o
mes far frem us housed some hundred & of refugees from a nearby province--
hardly any esoaped alive. From another place a stream of stretchers
brought in wounded to us. Our four doctors started at once to,operate,
two in eaeh reem,while I went along the stretahers picking out those who
hed a chanco of iife to go next into the operating room.Relatives were
pulling at my dress imploring me to let their particular loved one go
In next,loathe to ancpet the fact that they were beyond humnan aid. Oh,
it was ghastly! The heat!(We usually are on the hills at this time,but
our hills are in the battle area, and we are all needed in our stations.)
The suffering so patiently borne, the misory,the sight and smells made
me feel sick at heart and in body. One could only ask,"Dear Lord,how
long must this continue?"
The next day another raid before the poor victims of the
previous day had even recovered from the shock of the operations,and
everyone was busy with dressings. We notice that the victims suffer
most when the air raid sirens shriek over the ality. One small boy of
seven - se tiny - was on a boat when a bomb struek it and killed his
father and brothers in front of him, and injured his own log.whiah had
to be amputated. He breaks out in a sweat,and starts up crying when he
hears the warnings. An me! And we try to teach the world of brotherly
lov0.
It is a bitter thing to see Ameriean and British names on
bits of bombs picked up. Last week some American missionary friends
took pieces to the Consul and he agreed that they were from America.
An English author is here in Hankow this week and says that Britain
and Ameriea could stop the war in a week if they would anly get te-
gether,and that Ameriee is waiting for England to take the lead. Ob.

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.97)
nd see what we see surely she would take the lead right
forgive her if she doeen't!
Al preparations have been made fer the invasien er the eity.
lar authèrities kaaxmms want to drew off all their natiemals
but the missionaries have plainly stated their intentiem to
Our starf gets lower and lower because those whe can afferd it are
WAng for the farther inland previnces and taking their murse daughters
Nh them. I cannot blame them, as untold horrers have been perpetrated
 the civilians lert behind in oocupled cities. We have many high seheel
girls acting as practical aids, and thus we are carrying on,and intend
to do so whatever eemes,ir God keeps us.
My leave is due now. I could not possible leave at present
however,and must wait until God opens the way,which I hope may be next
spring,D.v. Our Buperintendent is a Chinese doctor and he needs his
English colleagues very urgently,and just now I am the senior one so
could not think of leaving.
One of the Hankow nursos who had to visit in a olinio in a
mueh-bombed area came baak one day after a raid with shaking hands and
 all unnorved.The next day we said to her,"Don't go today."But
she smiled and reoplied,"my Bible today said in my reading, 'I will fear
no evil while Thou art with me'".And ahe wont.
One of our deaconesses left Anhwei,a nearb provinge,with a
group of refugees on foot in the winter snow,and after an inoredible
Journey has only recently arrived here. For wouks they hid in the Moun-
tain saves where there was soarcely any food at all to be obtained.
They trekked unknown roads,lost the way,fell into swamps,wore hurried
aeross bridges by the soldiers just before they blew the bridgea up.
Several times they had to flee by bypaths to avoid the enemy had no
place to sleep nights,and finally arrived at one of our country stations
more dead than alive,and soarcely ruco nizable. Yet now she has recotered
and is leading meetings everywhere.She spoke to our nurses' Bible class
a week or so ago and her face shone as she said,"I just want to witness
to the fact that the grace of God ig sufficient for every ciroumstanceo
of life.Never once did He fail us on tiat long journey." She deseribe
how they all had to lie in the open at night she reminded them of how
Jesus had no plaoe to lay His head,and how they all cheered up and sang
hymms at the remembrance..Also how the Chinese soldiers meeting them at
the villages at night asked her to tell them of Jesus,and how glad she
was to de so. That is the sort of thing we are meeting all the time,and
it makes our hears glow that out of all this horror God is bringing a
new and sweeter life with deepened spiritual values out of it to this
great land,and who shall say what God has in the future!
The other day I came aaross two verses of a poem of John
Oxenham's in an old magasine and it seemed so true of China at present
that I pass it on to you to share.
THE DAWN
"I see a new world rising from the ashes of the past,
Fer when the soul of life blazed out at Freedom's trumpet blast,
The old,false gods of former days and things that made for shame
Are shriveled in the incense of that sacrificial flame.

Nov. 14, 1938.
Hon. Allen T. Treadway,
stookbridge, Mass.
Dear Mr. Treadway:
After personally meeting you at the Leverett Old
Home Day and after listening to your speech, I feel at
liberty to bring before you something which 1s troubling
me very much.Facts which were astounding were given
out from our pulpit yesterday concerning the Japanese
and Chinese confliot. We were told that Japan 1s very
poor in natural resources and 1f countries remalned
neutral who pretend to be neutral, the war couldn't go
on. U.8. 1s neutral and yet 4/5 of the gasoline con-
sumed there comes from United States. Their airplanes
which are doing so much harm 1n China are made in U.s.
The truoks which the Japanese are using are labelled
Ford, Chevrolet and Dodge. mere are they coming from?
A statement has come from China that if she were
Just fighting Japan, she thought she could win but she
couldn't combat Japan and United States too. If Japan
can conquer China, then she will be rich in natural
resources and won't need U.s.'s help and then she will
be in condition to turn on the United States.
This all boils down" to the fact that we have a
neutrality act and cannot our people be forced to live
by it?
I would like to see it done and I am appealing to
you to do your bit toward it.
Probably people who are pretending to be Christlans
are helping in this act of injustice because they have
made money their God 1nstead of God himself.
Thanking you for your patience in my complaint, I am
Respeotfully yours,
SUSIE A. JOHNSON.

Dear Mr. Secretary:
I am writing to express to you ny profound concern at the
buildiny up of Japanese military and navel resources vnich is result-
ing from the flov of munitions and of rew matericls from the United
894.24/470
States to Japan.
It seems to me & very terrible thing tht ve should in eny
way facilitate cnd support het the J&panese are doing in Chine. And
it seems to me only too likely thet Japan, if enabled, by our aid, to
mestor the enormous resources of Chine, and if driven still further
by the same fanatical sense of "menifest destiny" which is driving her
now, will seek to extend her domination to this side of the Pacific --
starting presumably in Centrel and South Americu. That we should in
eny way encourage Japan to pursue such : career seems to me & course of
extreordinery national folly -- folly which might well prove both fate-
ful and fetal.
I have noted with hearty epprovel the request of the Govern-
ment to the cirplane industry to disconti ue the filling of Japanese
military orders. I do not know what measure of discontinuance hes re-
sulted from thet request. If thet request hes prcved largely successful,
might not & similar request be addressed by the Government to all com-
panies now supplying Japen with munitions or rew meteriel of any sort?
Like millions of other Americcns, I am in the position of be-
TD
ing a stockholder in one or more companies which are presumably --
though not to my specific knowledge -- selling meterials to Japan and
deriving profits therefrom. The question is being raised more end more
frequently as to whether it might not be the duty of such stockholders
to seek to ascertain the facts, and if such sales are being made, to
protest to the compenies concerned.
This question might erise not only as regards individual
stockholders, but es regards institutions, such as that of which I have
the honor to be president, which derive a large proportion of their in-
come from a considerable variety of compenies.
F/WB
Yet it is quite clear that any efforts of mine as an individual

My dear Dr. Morgan:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of Novem-
ber 10, l9se, in regard to the export of war materials to
Japan.
894.24/471
There is transmitted herewith a copy of the Joint
Resolution of Congrems approved May 1, 1957, commonly re-
ferred to as the Noutrality Aot. It will be observed that
Seetion 1 (a) of this Joint Resolution provides that, when-
ever the President shall find that there exists a state of
war between or among two or more foreign states, he shall
proclaim such fact. Should the President issue a proolamm-
tion finding that a state of war exists between Chine and
Japan, the export from this country of arms, ammunition,
and implements of war to both China and Japan would be .
immediately prohibited. In the absence of sueh a proelame-
tion, there is no statutory authority for preventing sush
F
exports.
WB
In
Dr. Perry L. Morgan,
Morth Ridgeville, Ohio.

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.97)
8
In determining not to issue a proelamation, the Presi-
dent has exercised the disoretion vested in him by the
statute. He could not fail to recognise that the primary
purpose of this statute is to keep this country out of war,
and he, of course, felt obliged to take into account all
the pertinent conditions and ciroumstances. Inoidentally,
I mmy stress the fact, in view of what is above stated,
that a proelamation would have placed an embargo on the
shipment of the material mentioned not only to Japan but
to China.
The Noutrality Aot, moreover, does not authorize the
President or the Secretary of State under any oircumstances
to prohtbit the export of commodities other than arms, am-
munition, and implements of war. The artioles and materials
which are considered to be arms, ammunition, and implements
of war are enumerated in the President's Proclamation of
May 1, 1937, which is set forth under Part II of the en-
closed copy of the pamphlet Intermational Traffie in Arms.
You may be interested to know that since July 1,
1937, the Department of State has issued licenses for the
export of arms, ammmnition, and implements of war to China
in the value of $14,218,523.21 and to Japan in the value
of $10,798,865.88.

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
"HOSPITAL OF UNIVERSAL LOVE"
(Methodist General Hospital)
Hankow China. July 26,1938
ne of the refreshments of mind and soul after a terrible week
a good mail on Sunday. Oh,how I would like to have been with
In Kashmir! The very thought of it seems like a world apart from
this war horror.
After some weeks of rain and no raids,we have had a fortnight of
ghastly raids. One very near us last Tuesday with a thousand casualties
mongst the poor,and another that struck very near the American Church
Mission Hospital in Wuchang across the river Yangste. I went across to
see if I could help Miss Reiley there. A maternity hospital opened by the
givernment was struck. The bomb went through three floors of reinforced
conoeete,killing or wounding all the inmates in it.Some of the mothers
lay so peaceful in death with their babies clasped in their arms,whide
the resoue squad were trying to get bodies out from beneath the debris.
It seemed as though they were happier than the ones who were so griev-
ously wounded.Cne six months pregnant woman had a ghastly wound of the
abdomen,with rupture of the uterus,and a long slit in the buttock of the
tbetus.Did ever such devil's work darken civilization before!
Last Tuesday the bombs dropped so near us thatathe bonbers
passed over our heads,we waited in a tense stillness not knowing what
a moment might bring.But oh,so glad to be able to rest one's heart in
our loving Lord's care and feel Him near.Then we heard the thud,thud of
bombs falling,and then the explosions,and our hearts were pounding,know-
Ing that some poor folk were being killed somewhere near.A Guild Hall ro
not far from us housed some hundred s of refugees from a nearby province--
hardly any escaped alive. From another place a stream of stretchere
brought in wounded to us. Our four doctors started at once to,operate,
two in each room,while I went along the stretchers picking out those who
had a chance of life to go next into the operating room.Relatives were
pulling at my dress imploring me to let their particular loved one go
in next,loathe to accpet the fact that they were beyond humnan dd. Oh,
it was ghastly! The heat!(We usually are on the hills at thit time,but
our hills are in the battle area, and we are all needed in our stations.)
The suffering so patiently borne, the misery,the sight and smells made
me feel sick at heart and in body. One could only ask,"Dear Lord,how
long must this continue?"
The next day another raid before the poor victims of the
previous day had even recovered from the shock of the operations,and
everyone was busy with dressings. We notice that the victims suffer
most when the air raid sirens shriek over the city. One small boy of
seven - so tiny - was on a boat when a bomb struck it and killed his
father and brothers in front of him, and injured his own leg,which had
to be amputated. He breaks out in a sweat,and starts up crying when he
hears the warnings. Ah me! And we try to teach the world of brotherly
love
It is a bitter thing to see American and British names on
bits of bombs picked up. Last week some American missionary friends
took pieces to the Consul and he agreed that they were from America.
An English author is here in Hankow this week and says that Britain
and America could stop the war in a week if they would only get to-
gether,and that America is waiting for England to take the lead. Ob,

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.99)
2
Ad see what we see surely she would take the lead right
forgive her if she doesn't!
11 preparations have been made for the invasion of the city.
lar autherities maxmxmmts want to draw off all their nationals
but the missionaries have plainly stated their intention to
Our staff gets lower and lower because those who can afford it are
Ang for the farther inland provinces and taking their nurse daughters
th them. I cannot blame them, as untold horrors have been perpetrated
on the civilians left behind in occupied cities. We have many high school
girls acting as practioal aids, and thus we are carrying on,and intend
to do so whatever comes,if God keeps us.
My leave is due now. I could not possible leave at present
however,and must wait until God opens the way,which I hope may be next
spring,D.v. Our Superintendent is a Chinese doctor and he needs his
English colleagues very urgently,and just now I am the senior one so
could not think of leaving.
One of the Hankow nurses who had to visit in a clinic in a
much-bombed area came back one day after a raid with shaking hands and
she was all unnerved.The next day we said to her,"Don't go today."But
she smiled and replied,"My Bible today said in my reading, 'I will fear
no evil while Thou art with me'".And she went.
One of our deaconesses left Anhwei,a nearby provinge,with a
group of refugees on foot in the winter snow,and after an incredible
journey has only recently arrived here. For weeks they hid in the Moun-
tain waves where there was scarcely any food at all to be obtained.
They trekked unknown roads,lost the way,fell into swamps,were hurried
across bridges by the soldiers just before they blow the bridges up.
Several times they had to flee by bypaths to avoid the enemy,had no
place to sleep nights,and finally arrived at one of our country stations
more dead than alive,and scarcely recognizable. Yet now she has recotered
and is leading meetings everywhere.She spoke to our nurses' Bible class
a week or so ago and her face shone as she said,"I just want to witness
to the fact that the grace of God is sufficient for every circumstance
of life.Never once did He fail us on that long journey." She describe
how they all had to lie in the open at night she reminded them of how
Jesus had no place to lay His head,and how they all cheered up and sang
hymns at the remembrance..Also how the Chinese soldiers meeting them at
the villages at night asked her to tell them of Jesus,and how glad she
was to do so. That is the sort of thing we are meeting all the time,and
it makes our hears glow that out of all this horror God is bringing a
new and sweeter life with deepened spiritual values out of it to this
great land,and who shall say what God has in the future!
The other day I came across two verses of a poem of John
Oxenham's in an old magazine and it seemed so true of China at present
that I pass it on to you to share.
THE DAWN
"I see a now world rising from the ashes of the past,
For when the soul of life blazed out at Freedom's trumpet blast,
The old,false gods of former days and things that made for shame
Are shriveled in the incense of that sacrificial flame.

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.96)
3
dread times of suffering,from the shadowy vale of death,
life is springing which is quickened by our faith.
building God's new temples with our blood,our prayers,our tears,
o8
of grace that rise apace and shall outlast the years."
Goodbye....The joy of the Lord keep you ever young in Him.
Gladys Stephenson.

My dear Mrs. Panzer:
By reference from the White House I acknowledge the
reoeipt of your letter of October 23, 1938 1n regard to

As you doubtless are aware, Seotion 1 (a) of the
8

6
4

2

4/480
exists a state of war between or among two or more foreign
states he shall proolaim such fact. Should the President
1ssue a proclamation finding that a state of war exists
between China and Japan the export of arms, ammunition and
implements of war to both those countries would immediately
be prohibited by the terms of the Neutrality Aot. In the
abeenoe of such a proclamation, however, there exiets no
legal authority for preventing the export of arme either
to both those countries or to Japan alcne.

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.97)

The Neutrality Act, moreover, does not authorise the
President or the Secretary of State under any ciroua-
stanoes to prohibit the export of commodities other tham
arms, ammunition and implements of war. The artioles and
materials which are considered to be arms, ammunition and
implements of war are enumerated in the President's
Proclamation of May 1, 1937, wh1ch 1s set forth under
Part II of the enclosed copy of the pamphlet, International
Traffic in Arms.
I may add that the primary purpose of the Joint Reso-
lution of may 1, 1937, 1s to k ep this country out of war.
The question as to whether and when the provisions of the
Joint Resolut1on shall be invoked 1s properly left to the
sound discretion of the President. In deciding whether
he should invoke the Joint Resolution in a particular
situation the President must take into account various
factors and particularly the broad, general interests of
this country and its nationals. In the present situation
1n the Par East, the President has conoluded that, in the
light of all the facts and oircumstanoes, when weighed
from a practical point of view, it would not be to the
best interests of this country and our people as a
who1e

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.91)

whole to invoke the provisions of this Joint Resolu-
tiom.

sir:
Attention of Charles W. Yost, Assistant Chief,
Office of Arns and Munitions Control.
The recoipt is acknowledged of your letter of November 10, 1938,
addressed to Dr. W. J. Shermen, Chairman, Peace Committee of San Diego, in
answer to our letter and 983 signatures to a protest agninst the sending of
scrap iron to Japan.
We take note of the following points made in your letter, namely, :
If the President issues e proclamation that e state of war exists between
Jepan end China, shipment of arms to either country would be prohibited;
thet only those implements or war explicitly mentioned in the Neutrality Act
(scrap iron being omitted) would come under the authority of the President;
that since July 1937 the State Lepartment has issued licenses for the export
894.24/482
of arms to China to the value of over l4 million dollars, but to Japen only
something over ten million dollars.
As to the latter notation, do you not feel that these figures might
give a wrong impression ? If you add to your list the totel in ageregete,
which includes scrap iron, then the contribution of the United States to Japan's
total imports of essentiel war material is over 50%, end, if you compare the
contributions of such materiels sold by the United States to Japan with other
countries, our nation contributes 47.9% with the nearest competitor, England,
with 19%.
Do you not believe that, when a national policy is in retreat from the
pertinent facts of a critical situation, democracy is imperilled ? Since our
nation sold in 1937 over 35 millions of dollars in scrap iron to Japan,, that
would alter considerably the relative figures of l4 million dollars
and
l0 million dollers, would it not ?
And, since Japan has not made a formel declaration of war, may
no
conclude that it is not to her interest to do so ?
Putting aside the merits or demerits of the controversial Neutrality
Act, do you not believe that we would be justified in an embarg
and other essential war material on the following grounds : ? namely :
Unwillingness to be a partner in a ruthless war of conquest; to support
a country which has broken sacred treaties; to destroy our own interest and the
interests of the world in peace and security in the Pacific; to destroy our
future opportunities for legitimate trade in China, since China conquered means
China exploited by Japan; to strengthen and arm a nation, which would increase
the forces working against democracy; to furnish the means for an indefinite
long-drawn out conflict disturbing to the security of the world; to materially

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
2.
11-18-38
increame our taxes in arming to defend ourselves against a nation we are
ourselves arming; and our further unwillingness to break faith with those
spiritual and moral qualities of our nation, which are in revolt aginst the
inourgence of barbarism, and the traffic in arms, which furthers such
barbarism.
An embargo against scrap iron and other war material need not be
construed as an unfriendly act leading to war, any more than the public
and formal declarations against the inhumane persecutions of minorities by
Germany may be considered unfriendly. Is it not quite inconsistent, while
we are trying to find ways and means to take care of a few thousands of
refugees, we are helping to create millions of pitiful Chinese refugees by
supplying weapons to drive them from their homes ?
Nathaniel Peffer says "America must cease being the provisioner of
the Japanese army in an aggression abominable in itself, and working toward
America's injury.T
The means can be found and they must be found. And
organized efforts must be made to find them and to have them put into effect.
The thousands of signatures we are sending you are expressive of that sentiment.
Enclosed are lo76 signatures, and a copy of a resolution passed by
the San Diego County Ministerial Association.
Respectfully yours,
Shate H. Oeuring
(Mrs.) Kate H. Dewing,
fot the San Diego Peece
Committee, and the Women's International League for
Peace and Freedom.

"directly or indirectly.

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.99)
Whereas, the people of the United States desire peace and deplore international lawlessness and
aggression, and
Whereas, the Japanese invasion of China, fomented by the military party of Japan, has led the
United States of America and many other nations to indict Japan as the violator of the Nine-Power
Treaty and the Kellogg pact, and to protest repeatedly against the merciless bombing of undefended
Chinese cities, the unnecessary slaughter of Chinese civilians and the destruction and imperilment
of schools, churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in which their nationals are engaged, and
Whereas, I feel put to shame that American raw materials are being used to manufacture weapons
of war in Japan, that American planes are being used to carry bombs from America for the destruc-
tion of Chinese civilian population, that shells from America are loading Japanese guns, and that
America is supplying to Japan huge quantities of scrap iron, oil, trucks, and other materials used in
the furtherance of the war, over and above the amount of our normal trade of recent years, and
Whereas, I sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire to build up an independent, Demo-
cratic state, an end gravely threatened by the present aggression of Japan, and deplore the traffic
which makes it possible for certain American interests to profit from the wanton destruction of the
innocent victims of Japanese aggression,
Therefore, in spite of the almost irreparable crippling of China through the fall of Hankow and
Canton, I, the undersigned, respectfully petition the President of the United States and Congress,
in accordance with the principle of the Good Neighbor and in the interests of the people of the
United States, China, and Japan, to take every practical means, direct or indirect to bring to an end
a traffic from our country which is compelling us to be partner in the destruction of the Chinese
people.

something over l0 million dollars.

would alter considerably the relative figures of 14 millicn dollers
and
l0 million dollars, would it not ?
And, since Jepan hes not made a formsl declaration of war, may we not
conclude that it is not to her interest to do so ?
Putting aside the merits or demerits of the controversial Neutrality
Act, do you not believe that we would be justified in an embargo on scrap iron
end other essentiel wer meterial on the following grounds : ? nemely :
Unwillingness to be a partner in a ruthless war of conguest; to 
a country which has broken sacred treaties; to destroy our own interest and 
interests of the world in peace end security in the Pacific; to destroy e

=== Page 452 === (confidence 0.99)
Whereas Japan has day after day ruthlessly bombed
defended cities in China, resulting in uncounted deaths and wounds
mong non-combatants and untold destruction of property,
Whereas we believe that the Japanese government is
embarked on a policy of military aggression hostile to the interests
of the civilized countries of our world,
Whereas we believe that tne policy of the Japanese
government does not reflect the wishes f the character of the
Japanese people ana misrepresents tneir peace-loving qualities,
Whereas we are convinced t.at the United States of
America shares the guilt of Japan's invasion of China because of the
continued shipment of munitions and raw materials to Japan,
Be it therefore resolved tiat the government of the
United States immedlately adopt tne policy of non-participation in
aggression by imposing an embargo on exports to Japan of munitions
and war materials, including oil, gasoline, pig and scrap iron, air-
planes and airplane parts, machinery and engines, copper, automobiles
and automobile accessories, anu chemicals.

GRACE UNITED CHURCH OF THE MISSION

San Francisco, California
Nov. 13, 1938
The Honorable Richard J. Welch
House of Representatives
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
As our Congressman, you are interested in the appeal
which we make to you for world peace.
We of the Grace United Church School feel very deeply
the part the United States has taken in selling to Japan oil,
airplanes, and other war materials. By so doing we have
greatly aided in crushing the Chinese people: we have helped
Japan to win the war thus far!
We request you to use your influence as our Congress-
man to support those measures which would refuse war materials
to aggressor netions; realizing that your cooperation would
hasten the dawn of "Peace on Earth".
Lloyd L. Farley
Superintendent of Grace United Church School
Elisabeth B. Lewis
Secretary of Grace United Church School
